\mathbf{dg-cat}
, and the functor
\gamma
to be the identity, we have a factorization
(\operatorname{Re}_{W},\Sing_{W},\eta)
,

\operatorname{Re}_{W}:\Fun(\mathbf{dg-cat}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})\rightleftharpoons\mathbf{dg-cat}:\Sing_{W}

where we know that the right adjoint is given by
\Sing_{W}(X)=\Map(-,X)
.

We have constructed a chain of Quillen adjunctions from
\mathbf{dg-cat}
to
\Fun(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
. But, as we have remarked a few times before, we cannot compose right Quillen functors to left Quillen functors and still get a Quillen adjunction. And in this case, the adjunction
(l_{!},l^{*})
goes in the "wrong direction". Indeed, if we write the chain of Quillen adjunctions and we write the left Quillen functor always on top, we get the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(L_{W}\mathbf{dg-cat}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\mathbf{dg-cat}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{dg-cat}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Re}_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sing_{W}}

At this level there is nothing we can do to fix this: but on the homotopy categories there is. Indeed, in the homotopy categories we can construct a factorization of the functor
\Sing_{W}
, which would bypass the need for the adjoint
l_{!}
altogether. In other words, we are going to try and find a functor
\Sing^{\prime}
such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ho(\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ho(\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(L_{W}\mathbf{dg-cat}^{op},\mathbf{sSet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ho(\Fun(\mathbf{dg-cat}^{op},\mathbf{sSet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ho(\mathbf{dg-cat}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Re}_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sing_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sing^{\prime}}

commutes.

By definition of the simplicial localization category, if we have a functor
F:\mathbf{dg-cat}\to\mathbf{sSet}
that sends all morphisms in
W
to weak equivalences in
\mathbf{sSet}
, then it can be factorized through
l^{*}
on the homotopy categories. But
\Sing_{W}(X)=\Map(-,X)
is a right Quillen adjoint, so it sends weak equivalences between fibrant objects to weak equivalences, and all objects in
\mathbf{dg-cat}
are fibrant. So for all
X\in\mathbf{dg-cat}
, the image
\Sing_{W}(X)
can be factorized through
L_{W}\mathbf{dg-cat}
. That gives us a functorial factorization of the form

\Sing_{W}(X)=l^{*}\circ\Sing^{\prime}(X)

in the homotopy categories.

Now we only need to prove that the functors
\Sing=j^{*}\circ\Sing^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{Re}=\operatorname{Re}_{W}\circ l^{*}\circ j_{!}
are truly adjoints, i.e. that for all
X\in\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
and for all
Y\in\mathbf{dg-cat}
there exists an functorial isomorphism of the form

\left[X,j^{*}\circ\Sing^{\prime}(Y)\right]_{\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}\simeq\left[\operatorname{Re}_{W}\circ l^{*}\circ j_{!}(X),Y\right]_{\mathbf{dg-cat}}.

Let us start on the left side and work our way through. We start with
\left[X,j^{*}\circ\Sing^{\prime}(Y)\right]_{\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}
. As the functors
j_{!}
and
j^{*}
are adjoints, we have that

\left[X,j^{*}\circ\Sing^{\prime}(Y)\right]_{\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}\simeq\left[j_{!}(X),\Sing^{\prime}(Y)\right]_{\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(L_{W}\mathbf{dg-cat}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}.

Because we are working on the homotopy categories, by the definition of simplicial localizations (see Definition
3.3.42
) the functor
l^{*}
is fully faithful. That means, in particular, that we get the following isomorphism:

\left[j_{!}(X),\Sing^{\prime}(Y)\right]_{\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(L_{W}\mathbf{dg-cat}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}\simeq\left[l^{*}\circ j_{!}(X),\Sing_{W}(Y)\right]_{\Fun(\mathbf{dg-cat}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}.

And finally, using the definition of an adjunction again on
\operatorname{Re}_{W}
and
\Sing_{W}
, we get that

\left[l^{*}\circ j_{!}(X),\Sing_{W}(Y)\right]_{\Fun(\mathbf{dg-cat}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}\simeq\left[\operatorname{Re}_{W}\circ l^{*}\circ j_{!}(X),Y\right]_{\mathbf{dg-cat}}.

We have the isomorphism

\left[X,\Sing(Y)\right]_{\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})}\simeq\left[\operatorname{Re}(X),Y\right]_{\mathbf{dg-cat}}

and the pair
\operatorname{Re}:\Ho(\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet}))\rightleftharpoons\Ho(\mathbf{dg-cat}):\Sing
is an adjunction on the homotopy categories. We have finished the proof.

∎

We have now an adjunction between the categories we wanted itfor. The next step is proving that this functor is an equivalence. But it is not that easy. In order to do that, we need a few background concepts and constructions.

4.2
dg-Segal spaces

As we said in the introduction, we take our inspiration for this section from complete Segal spaces. We remind the reader that the definition of said spaces is the following.

Definition 4.2.1
.

[
Rez01
, Def. 4.1]
Let
W
be a Reedy fibrant simplicial space. We say that
W
is a
Segal space
if the maps

W_{k}\to\overbrace{W_{1}\times_{W_{0}}\ldots\times_{W_{0}}W_{1}}^{\text{$k$ times}}

are weak equivalences for all
k\geq 2
.

We can put some similar conditions on our functors, but in our case they won’t be enough. Indeed, we aren’t just working with simplicial sets: we have a linear structure to worry about. Consequently, we need an additional condition on the shift, and for that, we are going to define the action of adding a module to a complex of modules.

Definition 4.2.2
.

Let
G\in Gr(\mathbf{C(k)})
be a graph in the category of complexes,
x,y\in\Ob(G)
two objects in
G
, and
\alpha\in Z^{n}(G(x,y))
a cycle in
G(x,y)
. We define the graph
G(<\alpha>)
to be a graph of complexes such that

—

The graph
G(<\alpha>)
has the same objects as
G
.

—

The graph
G(<\alpha>)
has the same morphisms as
G
between
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\Ob(G)
if
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\neq(x,y)
, i.e.

G(<\alpha>)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=G(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.

—

We define
G(<\alpha>)(x,y)
to be the complex of morphisms
G(x,y)\oplus_{k}\beta
where
d\beta=\alpha
.

In other words, we have that
G(<\alpha>)
is a pushout in the graphs over the morphism
k[n]\to k^{c}[n]
, where
k[n]
is the graph with two objects,
0,1
, and
k[n]
as
\Hom(0,1)
the morphism between the two, and
k^{c}[n]
is the graph with two objects,
0,1
, and the complex
\Hom(0,1)
which is always zero except for the degrees
n-1
and
n
, where it is
k
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{c}[n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(<\alpha>).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Remark 4.2.3
.

It isn’t hard to see that all we have done here has been adding a term in degree
n-1
to the complex of modules
G(x,y)
.

Now that we have this definition, we can apply it to finally define the conditions of our image.

Definition 4.2.4
.

Let
F\in\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
be a simplicial functor from the cofibrant free dg-categories to the simplicial sets. We say that
F
satisfies the
dg-Segal conditions
if:

1.

For all
L,K\in c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
,
F(L\coprod K)\to F(L)\times F(K)
is a weak equivalence.

2.

The image of the initial object is a point, i.e.
F(\emptyset)\simeq*
.

3.

Let
G
be a graph in
Gr(\mathbf{C(k)})
and
x,y\in\Ob(G)
. For all
\alpha\in Z^{n}(G(x,y))
, the image of the free dg-category issued from
G(<\alpha>)
is a homotopy pullback in
\mathbf{sSet}
of the following form:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(L(G(<\alpha>)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(L(G))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,n,1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\Delta_{k}(1,n,1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner^{h}}

where
\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,n,1)=L(k^{c}[n])
and
\Delta_{k}(1,n,1)=L(k[n])
; i.e.
F
sends the homotopy pushouts of the previous definition to homotopy pullbacks.

We denote the full subcategory of
F\in\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
that satisfies the dg-Segal conditions by
\mathbf{dg-Segal}
and call its objects
dg-Segal spaces
.

Our conjecture at this point is that the image of the functor
\Sing
we defined in Section 4.1 is formed up to weak equivalence of the functors that satisfy the dg-Segal conditions. In order to prove that, first we need to prove that every object in the image is of this form.

Proposition 4.2.5
.

Let
T\in\mathbf{dg-cat}
be a dg-category. Then the functor
\Sing(T)
satisfies the dg-Segal conditions.

Proof.

We have to see that
T
fulfills the three conditions of the definition.

1. Let
L,K\in c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
be two cofibrant free dg-categories. As
L,K
are cofibrant, we have that

\Sing(T)(L\coprod K)=\Map(L\coprod K,T)=\Hom(L\coprod K,C_{*}(T)).

But by definition of coproduct, the condition 1. holds in this case:

\Hom(L\coprod K,C_{*}(T))=\Hom(L,C_{*}(T))\times\Hom(K,C_{*}(T))=\Map(L,T)\times\Map(K,T)

and we have finished.

2. This condition is evident:
\Sing(\emptyset)=\Map(-,\emptyset)=*
by definition of final object.

3. We need to prove that the following diagram is a homotopy pullback
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sing(T)(L(G(<\alpha>)))=\Map(L(G(<\alpha>)),T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sing(T)(L(G))=\Map(L(G),T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sing(T)(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,n,1))=\Map(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,n,1),T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sing(T)(\Delta_{k}(1,n,1))=\Map(\Delta_{k}(1,n,1),T).}

All these objects are in
\mathbf{sSet}
, which is a proper category, which means that if one of these arrows is a fibration, then we have a homotopy pullback. By Theorem
3.4.9
, we have that
\Delta_{k}(1,s,1)\to\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,s,1)
is a generating cofibration in
\mathbf{dg-cat}
, and by Proposition
3.2.55
,
\Sing(T)(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,s,1))\to\Sing(T)(\Delta_{k}(1,s,1))
is a fibration. We then have that the previous diagram is a homotopy pullback.

The functor
\Sing(T)
satisfies the dg-Segal conditions and we have finished our proof.

∎

Remark 4.2.6
.

We draw the attention of our readers to the fact that, even though we haven’t asked for dg-Segal spaces to be fibrant, by adjointness every
\Sing(T)
is actually fibrant.

We have proven that every element in the image of
\Sing
is a dg-Segal space. We ask the reader to keep that in mind for when we have to prove the essential surjectivity.

But for now, let us focus on the model structure we can get for these dg-Segal spaces. In
[
Rez01
]
, Rezk takes his model structure over
\Fun(\Delta^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
and does a Bousfield localization that makes the Segal spaces into its fibrant objects; he calls that
the Segal space model category structure
. Following his footsteps, we get a new model structure for
\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
where the fibrant objects are the dg-Segal spaces that are fibrant for the projective structure. Even though the results about Segal and complete Segal spaces in this section are due to Rezk, we will take inspiration in Rasekh’s lecture notes in
[
Ras20
]
for their presentation.

Theorem 4.2.7
.

There exists a simplicial closed model structure on
\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
and a class of morphisms
C
such that

1.

The cofibrations are the same as in the projective model structure.

2.

The fibrant objects are the dg-Segal spaces which are fibrant for the projective model structure.

3.

The weak equivalences are the
C
-local equivalences with respect to the class
C
.

We call such a model structure
the dg-Segal model structure.

Proof.

In order to prove this, we are going to utilize the left Bousfield localization. The first thing we need to do is find a class of morphisms
C
such that the dg-Segal spaces are exactly the
C
-local objects, i.e. such that for every dg-Segal space
F
and for every morphism
f:A\to B
in
C
, the morphism

\Map(B,F)\to\Map(A,F)

is a weak equivalence. For that, we define three classes of morphisms, one for each condition in the definition of a dg-Segal space.

1. Our first class of morphisms is
C_{1}=\{\Sing(L)\coprod\Sing(L^{\prime})\to\Sing(L\coprod L^{\prime})/\ L,L^{\prime}\in c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}\}
. Let us check that the
C_{1}
-local objects are exactly the functors that satisfy the first condition of the definition of dg-Segal spaces. Let
F
be a functor in
\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
that is a
C_{1}
-local object. Then, by the Yoneda Lemma, we have that

\Map(\Sing(L\coprod L^{\prime}),F)\simeq F(L\coprod L^{\prime})\to\Map(\Sing(L)\coprod\Sing(L^{\prime}),F)\simeq F(L)\times F(L^{\prime})

is a weak equivalence. By construction,
F
satisfies the first dg-Segal condition.

2. As the second condition of the definition of a dg-Segal space is just one single weak equivalence, the class of morphisms associated to it will also have just one morphism. We consider
C_{2}=\{\emptyset\to\Sing(\emptyset\}
. Let
F
be a
C_{2}
-local object. Then, also by the Yoneda lemma, we have that

\Map(\Sing(\emptyset),F)\simeq F(\emptyset)\to\Map(\emptyset,F)\simeq*

is a weak equivalence, and
F
satisfies the second dg-Segal condition.

3. Lastly, we take the class
C_{3}=\{\Sing(L(G))\coprod_{\Sing(\Delta_{k}(1,s,1))}\Sing(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,s,1))\to\Sing(L(G(<\alpha>))/\ G\in Gr(\mathbf{C(k)}),s\in\mathbb{Z},x,y\in\Ob(G),\alpha\in Z^{n}(x,y)\}
. Let
F
be a
C_{3}
-local object. The, by the Yoneda lemma, we have that the morphism

\Map(\Sing(L(G(<\alpha>))),F)\simeq F(L(G(<\alpha>)))\to

\Map(\Sing(L(G))\coprod_{\Sing(\Delta_{k}(1,n,1))}\Sing(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,n,1)),F)\simeq F(L(G))\times_{\Sing(\Delta_{k}(1,n,1))}F(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,n,1))

is a weak equivalence. Alternatively, that means that the diagram in condition 3 of the dg-Segal condition is a homotopy pullback and
F
satisfies the third dg-Segal condition.

We take the class of morphisms
C=C_{1}\cup C_{2}\cup C_{3}
to be our
C
in the Bousfield localization. If such a localization exists, its fibrant objects will be exactly the dg-Segal spaces which are fibrant for the projective model structure.

We have by Theorem
3.2.71
that if the category we are trying to localize is a left proper cellular model category, then the left Bousfield localization exists. We know from Proposition
3.2.72
that the category of simplicial sets
\mathbf{sSet}
is left proper and cellular, and from Proposition
3.2.73
that the functors on it are also left proper and cellular. So this localization exists and we have finished.

∎

Considering how we have followed Rezk’s method pretty closely, it won’t be surprising to our readers to see that there is a close relationship between our dg-Segal spaces and classic Segal spaces. Indeed, there is a Quillen adjoint between the model category
\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
and the model category
\Fun(\Delta^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
. Let us construct that.

Proposition 4.2.8
.

There exists a morphism, called
the linearisation of
\Delta
, between the categories
\Delta
and
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
, and it defines a Quillen adjunction between the categories
\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
and
\Fun(\Delta^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
with their respective projective structures.

Proof.

Let
[n]\in\Delta
be an object in
\Delta
. We define
j([n])=[n]\times k
to be a free dg-category such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j([n])=0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

This is a free dg-category of finite type, and it is also cofibrant (for a detailed proof of the cofibrancy, see Corollary
5.2.6
), so this morphism
j
is well-defined as
\Delta\to c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
. We construct then the following Quillen adjunction:

j_{!}:\Fun(\Delta^{op},\mathbf{sSet})\simeq\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(\Delta^{op},\mathbf{sSet})\rightleftharpoons\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}^{op}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathbf{sSet}):j^{*}

and we have finished our proof.

∎

Notation 4.2.9
.

We denote the images by the linearization morphism by
j(\left[n\right])=\Delta_{k}(n,0,1)
.

Definition 4.2.10
.

We call the morphism
j^{*}:\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}^{op}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathbf{sSet})\to\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(\Delta^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
the
delinearlisation morphism
.

Now, we have calculated that Quillen adjunction for the projective model structure. But we have two localizations here: let us prove that this stays a Quillen adjunction in the localizations. Let us see that this adjunction sends dg-Segal spaces to classic Segal spaces.

Proposition 4.2.11
.

Let
F\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
be a dg-Segal space. Then its image by the delinearisation morphism
j^{*}
is a Segal space.

Proof.

Let
F
be a dg-Segal space. Then, in order to prove that its image by
j^{*}
is a Segal space, by definition of Segal space we need to prove that for all
n\geq 1
, the morphism

j^{*}(F)_{n}\to\overbrace{j^{*}(F)_{1}\times_{j^{*}(F)_{0}}\ldots\times_{j^{*}(F)_{0}}j^{*}(F)_{1}}^{\text{$n$ times}}

is a weak equivalence. If we unravel that definition, we have that for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
j^{*}(F)_{i}=j^{*}(F)([i])=F(j([i]))=F(\Delta_{k}(i,0,1))
. So proving that
j^{*}(F)
is a Segal space can be rewritten as asking that for all
n\geq 1
,

\Phi_{n}:F(\Delta_{k}(n,0,1))\to F(\Delta_{k}(1,0,1))\times_{F(\Delta_{k}(0,0,1))}\ldots\times_{F(\Delta_{k}(0,0,1))}F(\Delta_{k}(1,0,1))

is a weak equivalence of simplicial spaces.

In order to simplify the notation, we will denote
\Delta_{k}(i,0,1)
by
\Delta_{k}^{i}
. We remark too that
F(\Delta_{k}^{0})=F(k)
.

We will prove the proposition by induction.

—

n=1
. This is obvious, since
F(\Delta_{k}^{1})\simeq F(\Delta_{k}^{1})
. There is nothing to prove.

—

n\geq 2
. We assume that the morphism

\Phi_{n-1}:F(\Delta_{k}^{n-1})\to\overbrace{F(\Delta_{k}^{1})\times_{F(k)}\ldots\times_{F(k)}F(\Delta_{k}^{1})}^{\text{$n-1$ times}}

is a weak equivalence. Let us prove that the morphism
\Phi_{n}
is also a weak equivalence.

As usual for inductions, we have to decompose
F(\Delta_{k}^{n})
in a way that makes
F(\Delta_{k}^{n-1})
appear. In this case, we will use the properties of a dg-Segal category to do so. We define
G^{0}
to be a graph of the following form:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{0}=j([n-1])\coprod*=0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n-1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Then, we can construct
\Delta_{k}^{n}
as the following pushout:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{k}(1,-1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,-1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(G^{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{k}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

By using the third dg-Segal condition, we can write
F(\Delta_{k}^{n})
in the following way:

F(\Delta_{k}^{n})\simeq F(G^{0})\times_{F(\Delta_{k}(1,-1,1))}F(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,-1,1))\simeq F(\Delta_{k}^{n-1}\coprod k)\times_{F(\Delta_{k}(1,-1,1))}F(k\coprod k)).

By the first dg-Segal condition, we can make those coproducts commute with
F
in the following way:

F(\Delta_{k}^{n})\simeq F(\Delta_{k}^{n-1}\coprod k)\times_{F(\Delta_{k}(1,-1,1))}F(k\coprod k))\simeq(F(\Delta_{k}^{n-1})\times F(k))\times_{F(\Delta_{k}(1,-1,1))}(F(k)\times F(k)).

Now, in particular, if we take
n=1
, we get the following formula:

F(\Delta_{k}^{1})\simeq(F(\Delta_{k}^{0})\times F(k))\times_{F(\Delta_{k}(1,-1,1))}F(k)^{2}=F(k)^{2}\times_{F(\Delta_{k}(1,-1,1))}F(k)^{2}.

We are almost there. If we add and subtract one
F(k)
to the formula of
F(\Delta_{k}^{n})
we will be done.

F(\Delta_{k}^{n})\simeq(F(\Delta_{k}^{n-1})\times F(k))\times_{F(\Delta_{k}(1,-1,1))}F(k)^{2}\simeq F(\Delta_{k}^{n-1})\times_{F(k)}(F(k)\times F(k))\times_{F(\Delta_{k}(1,-1,1))}F(k)^{2}

and we have that
F(\Delta_{k}^{n})\simeq F(\Delta_{k}^{n-1})\times_{F(k)}F(\Delta_{k}^{1})
. By the induction hypothesis, we have

F(\Delta_{k}^{n})\simeq\overbrace{F(\Delta_{k}^{1})\times_{F(\Delta_{k}^{0})}\ldots\times_{F(\Delta_{k}^{0})}F(\Delta_{k}^{1})}^{\text{$n-1$ times}}\times_{F(\Delta_{k}^{0})}F(\Delta_{k}^{1}).

The image
j^{*}(F)
is a Segal space and we have finished our proof.

∎

Remark 4.2.12
.

The reader is probably wondering why we introduced the notation
\Delta_{k}(n,0,1)
only to immediately simplify it. The answer is that those dg-categories are actually a particular case of some dg-categories we will denote
\Delta_{k}(n,s,d)
, where
s\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
d\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, which we think might form a full subcategory of
c\mathcal{L}
that would be sufficient for this construction. We will expand on this hypothesis in Section
5.2
of Chapter
5
.

Corollary 4.2.13
.

The adjunction
j_{!}:\Fun(\Delta^{op},\mathbf{sSet})\rightleftharpoons\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}^{op}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathbf{sSet}):j^{*}
is a Quillen adjunction for the Segal and dg-Segal model structures, respectively.

Proof.

This result is a direct consequence of the last proposition. Indeed, by Proposition
3.1.16
, we know that if we have an adjunction between two model categories
F:M\rightleftharpoons N:G
and we want to prove it is a Quillen adjunction, then we only need to prove that
F
preserves cofibrations and
G
preserves fibrant objects.

Now, the left Bousfield localization doesn’t change cofibrations, and our adjunction was already a Quillen adjunction on the projective model structure;
j_{!}
preserves cofibrations. We only have left to prove that
j^{*}
preserves fibrant objects. But the fibrant objects in the dg-Segal model structure are the dg-Segal spaces that were fibrant in the projective model structure. By the last proposition,
j^{*}
sends dg-Segal spaces to Segal spaces, and because it is already a Quillen adjunction in the projective model structure, it preserves fibrant objects in that structure. So it preserves fibrant objects in the dg-Segal model structure.

The adjunction is a Quillen adjunction on the Segal and dg-Segal model structures, respectively, and we have finished our proof.

∎

4.3
Complete dg-Segal spaces

When trying to characterize
\infty
-categories using Segal spaces, we realize quickly that the definition of Segal spaces that has been already given is not enough. Indeed, there is a class of morphisms, called Dwyer-Kan morphisms, that should be equivalences but aren’t. In
[
Rez01
]
, in his quest to invert those, the author gets to the following result:

Definition 4.3.1
.

We define
E(1)
to be the discrete space given at level
n\in\mathbb{N}
by

E(1)_{n}=\{x,y\}^{[n]},

i.e. by two non-degenerate cells on each level. Those are given by
(xy)^{n/2}
and
(yx)^{n/2}
if
n
is odd and
(xy)^{(n-1)/2}
and
(yx)^{(n-1)/2}
if
n
is odd.

Proposition 4.3.2
.

[
Rez01
, Prop. 7.6]
,
[
Ras20
, Prop. 4.5]
Let
f:V\to W
be a map between two Segal spaces. We assume that the morphism

\Map(E(1),X)\to\Map(*,X)

is a weak equivalence, with both
X=V
and
W
, for a certain morphism
*\to E(1)
. Then
f
is a Dwyer-Kan equivalence if and only if it is a weak equivalence.

But that just means that the Dwyer-Kan equivalences are equivalences if they are between two
C
-local objects in
\mathbf{sSet}
, where
C
is a class with one object,
*\to E(1)
. So they define a new concept, that of complete Segal spaces, which are just the objects we have just defined.

Definition 4.3.3
.

[
Rez01
, Def. 4.1, Section 6]

[
Ras20
, Prop. 4.5]
Let
W
be a Reedy fibrant simplicial space. We say that
W
is a
complete Segal space
if the maps

W_{k}\to\overbrace{W_{1}\times_{W_{0}}\ldots\times_{W_{0}}W_{1}}^{\text{$k$ times}}\text{ and }\Map(E(1),W)\to\Map(*,W)

are weak equivalences for all
k\geq 1
. Or, equivalently,

W_{k}\to\overbrace{W_{1}\times_{W_{0}}\ldots\times_{W_{0}}W_{1}}^{\text{$k$ times}}\text{ and }W_{0}\to W_{hoequiv}

are weak equivalences for all
k\geq 1
and
W_{hoequiv}
the space of homotopy equivalences.

And now Rezk uses the left Bousfield localization with
\{*\to E(1)\}
as his
C
and the complete Segal spaces as his
C
-local objects in order to have a model category that works for him.

Theorem 4.3.4
.

[
Rez01
]
There exists a simplicial closed model structure on the category of simplicial spaces, with the following properties:

1.

The cofibrations are precisely the monomorphisms.

2.

The fibrant objects are precisely the complete Segal spaces.

3.

The weak equivalences are precisely the Dwyer-Kan equivalences between complete Segal spaces.

In our case we will see that, mirroring the classic situation, the dg-Segal spaces we have defined are also not enough to completely characterize our dg-categories. Indeed, we can’t prove that the functor
\Sing
is fully faithful; we will be able to do so up to a certain morphism, that we will call a DK-equivalence. As such, we will need to do another left Bousfield localization in order to invert those, and then define its local objects to be our complete dg-Segal spaces.

Instead of doing it in that order, though, we will start with the definition of the complete dg-Segal model structure. For that, we will use the linearisation functor that we defined on the last section.

Definition 4.3.5
.

Let
E(1)
be as defined above. We define

E_{k}=\mathbb{L}(j_{!}(E(1))).

Remark 4.3.6
.

We warn our readers of the fact that
E_{k}
is not the image of a dg-category through our functor
\Sing
; in fact, it isn’t even a dg-Segal space!

Theorem 4.3.7
.

There exists a class of morphisms
C
and a simplicial closed model structure on
\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
such that

1.

The cofibrations are the same as in the projective model structure.

2.

The fibrant objects are the dg-Segal spaces which satisfy that
F(k)\to j^{*}(F)_{hoequiv}
is a weak equivalence of simplicial spaces and are fibrant for the projective model structure.

3.

The weak equivalences are the
C
-local equivalences.

We call such a model structure
the complete dg-Segal model structure.

Proof.

The proof of this is straightforward, as we are just transporting the complete Segal model structure to our new setting via the linearisation functor. Indeed, we can construct this by using a left Bousfield localization on the dg-Segal model structure by the class
C=\{\Phi:E_{k}\to\Sing(k)\}=\{\Phi:j_{!}(E(1))\to j_{!}(*)\}
. We won’t write down the details about whether this gives us an actual localization, as they are the exact same as in the construction of the dg-Segal model structure.

The only thing left to do is to see that the
C
-local objects are really as we have defined them above. Let
F
be a
C
-local object. We have that
F
is a dg-Segal space that is fibrant for the projective structure, and also that the following morphism

\Map(\Sing(k),F)\to\Map(E_{k},F)

is a weak equivalence. By the definition of an adjunction, we have that

\Map(E_{k},F)=\Map(j_{!}(E(1)),F)\simeq\Map(E(1),j^{*}(F))\text{ and }\Map(\Sing(k),F)=\Map(j_{!}(*),F)\simeq\Map(*,j^{*}(F)).

So the condition is equivalent to asking for
\Map(*,j^{*}(F))\to\Map(E(1),j^{*}(F))
to be a weak equivalence. And that is exactly the definition of
j^{*}(F)
being a complete Segal space. So if
F
is a
C
-local object, then
F
is a dg-Segal space such that
j^{*}(F)_{0}=F(k)\to j^{*}(F)_{hoequiv}
is a weak equivalence. By Proposition
3.2.76
, a fibrant object for the complete dg-Segal model structure is such an
F
which is also fibrant for the projective model structure.

We have all the conditions we needed and our proof is complete.

∎

Remark 4.3.8
.

It is important to remember that
j^{*}(F)_{hoequiv}
is the subset of
j^{*}(F)_{1}
whose 0-simplexes are homotopy equivalences. Considering we’ve already seen that
j^{*}(F)_{1}=F(\Delta_{k}(1,0,1))
, we can rewrite
j^{*}(F)_{hocolim}
as the subset of
F(\Delta_{k}(1,0,1))
whose 0- simplexes are homotopy equivalences, with no mentions of the delinearisation morphism.

Notation 4.3.9
.

Let
F
be a dg-Segal space. We denote the subset of
F(\Delta_{k}(1,0,1))
whose 0- simplexes are homotopy equivalences by
F_{hoequiv}
.

Definition 4.3.10
.

Let
F
be an object in
\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
. We say that
F
is
a complete dg-Segal space
if it is a dg-Segal space and the morphism

F(k)\to F_{hoequiv}

is a weak equivalence.

Definition 4.3.11
.

We denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{dg-Segal}
of complete dg-Segal spaces by
\mathbf{dg-Segal_{c}}
.

As we did with the dg-Segal spaces, we will prove now that every element of
Sing
is actually a complete dg-Segal space.

Proposition 4.3.12
.

Let
T\in\mathbf{dg-cat}
be a dg-category. Then
\Sing(T)
is a complete dg-Segal space.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the proof of Corollary 8.7 in
[
Toë07
]
. Indeed, during that proof Toën proves that for all
T\in\mathbf{dg-cat}
, the morphism
\Map(k,T)=\Sing(T)(k)\to\Map(\Delta_{k}(1,0,1),T)=\Sing(T)(\Delta_{k}(1,0,1))
induces an injection on
\pi_{0}
and a bijection on
\pi_{i}
for all
i>0
, and that its image in the homotopy category are the morphisms of
\left[T\right]
that are isomorphisms. That means that the morphism is fully faithful, and that its essential image is
\Sing(T)_{hoequiv}
.

So for all
T
, the morphism
\Sing(T)(k)\to\Sing(T)_{hoequiv}
is a weak equivalence, and
\Sing(T)
is a complete dg-Segal space.

∎

Now, we have a nice definition of our
C
-local objects, but we still have the
C
-local equivalences defined in an abstract manner. and we said before that we were going to define something called the DK-equivalences that we needed to make into equivalences. Before we do that, though, we need one additional definition.

Notation 4.3.13
.

We denote the full subcategory of cofibrant complexes of modules by
\mathbf{C(k)}^{c}\subset\mathbf{C(k)}
.

Definition 4.3.14
.

Let
F\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
be a functor that satisfies the dg-Segal conditions, let
x,y\in\pi_{0}(F(k))
. We define
F_{(x,y)}\in\Fun(\mathbf{C(k)}^{c,op},\mathbf{sSet})
to be, for all
E\in\mathbf{C(k)}^{c}
cofibrant complexes of modules, the homotopy fiber of
F(E_{x,y})\to F(k)\times F(k)
where
E_{x,y}
is the free dg-category given by the graph with two objects,
x
and
y
, and
E
as the complex between
x
and
y
,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{(x,y)}(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(E_{x,y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(k)\times F(k).}

We call
F_{(x,y)}

the dg-mapping space of
F
at
x,y
.

This definition is more intuitive than we may think. Indeed, these functors are always representable.

Proposition 4.3.15
.

Let
F\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
be a functor that satisfies the dg-Segal conditions, let
x,y\in\pi_{0}(F(k))
. There exists a unique complex of modules up to weak equivalence
F(x,y)\in\mathbf{C(k)}
such that
F_{(x,y)}(-)\simeq\Map(-,F(x,y))
.

Proof.

The uniqueness is just a consequence of the Yoneda lemma. We assume the existence of two complexes of modules,
F_{1}(x,y)
and
F_{2}(x,y)
, such that
F_{(x,y)}(-)\simeq\Map(-,F_{1}(x,y))
and also
F_{(x,y)}(-)\simeq\Map(-,F_{2}(x,y))
. But the Yoneda lemma tells us that a weak equivalence of representable presheafs must come from a weak equivalence on the representing objects. Which means that we have

F_{1}(x,y)\simeq F_{2}(x,y).

We have the uniqueness.

Let us see the existence next. This is a direct consequence of Proposition 1.9 in
[
TV15
]
, in particular of a certain point of the proof of part 1. Indeed, we prove there that if
A
is an
\infty
-category, then all functor
G\in\Fun(A^{op},\mathbf{sSet})
that commutes with homotopy colimits is representable. That result has, then, reduced our problem to proving that the functor
F_{(x,y)}
sends all homotopy colimits to homotopy limits (we remind the reader that
F_{(x,y)}
is a contravariant functor). In order to make the writing of this proof easier, we will ignore the fact that
F
is contravariant and call this "commuting with homotopy limits".

Now, proving that a functor commutes with homotopy limits can be done by proving that it commutes with all filtered homotopy limits, all homotopy pushouts, and all finite sums. As
F
satisfies the dg-Segal conditions, it already commutes with all filtered limits along perfect objects, which means that
F_{(x,y)}
commutes with all filtered limits; and by the third dg-Segal condition it already commutes with homotopy pushouts along generating cofibrations. As we can write every homotopy pushout as a filtered homotopy limit over a homotopy pushout along a generating cofibration (in this case, the projective model structure), that means that
F_{(x,y)}
commutes with all homotopy pushouts. Finally, all finite sums are homotopy pushouts except for the null sum, which means that is all we have left to prove.

Let
G
be a graph with two objects and the null complex between them. By definition, the free dg-category associated to
G
is the coproduct
L(G)=k\coprod k
. By the first dg-Segal condition, that means that
F(L(G))=F(k\coprod k)\simeq F(k)\times F(k)
. If we apply now the definition of
F_{(x,y)}
, we have that
F_{(x,y)}(G)
is the homotopy fiber of
F(L(G))=F(k)\times F(k)\to F(k)\times F(k)
. As this is an isomorphism, we have that
F_{(x,y)}(G)=*
, the null object in
\mathbf{sSet}
, and we are done.

We have proven, then, that
F_{(x,y)}
sends all homotopy colimits to homotopy limits. We have that
F_{(x,y)}
is representable, i.e. there exists a complex of modules
F(x,y)
such that
F_{(x,y)}(-)\simeq\Map(-,F(x,y))
.

And we have finished this proof.

∎

Remark 4.3.16
.

By the Yoneda lemma again, it is obvious that if the functor
F
is of the form
\Sing(T)
with
T\in\mathbf{dg-cat}
, then we have that for all
E\in\mathbf{C(k)}^{c}
,

F_{(x,y)}(E)=\Map(E,T(x,y)).

And one last definition.

Definition 4.3.17
.

Let
F\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
be a dg-Segal space. We call the
homotopy category of
F
, and we denote it by
\left[F\right]
, the category whose objects are the 0-simplexes of
F(k)
and whose morphisms are given for all
x,y\in\pi_{0}(F(k))
by

\left[F\right](x,y)=H^{0}(F(x,y))

i.e. the cohomology groups of degree 0 of the complex of morphisms associated to the dg-mapping space at
x
and
y
.

Remark 4.3.18
.

We point to the reader that we have that if
F\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
, then

H^{0}(F(x,y))=\pi_{0}(\map_{j^{*}(F)}(x,y)),

where
\map_{j^{*}(F)}
is the mapping space in the associated Segal space, so we can use the composition law there.

We are now ready to define DK-equivalences.

Definition 4.3.19
.

Let
f:F\to G\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
be a morphism between two functors satisfying the dg-Segal conditions. We say that
f
is
a DK-equivalence
if it satisfies the following conditions:

1.

The induced morphism
\left[f\right]:\left[F\right]\to\left[G\right]
is essentially surjective.

2.

For all objects
x,y\in\pi_{0}(F(k))
, the induced morphism on the dg-mapping spaces,
F_{(x,y)}\to G_{(f(x),f(y))}
, is a quasi-equivalence of functors in
\Fun(\mathbf{C(k)}^{c,op},\mathbf{sSet})
.

Remark 4.3.20
.

Some readers might ask themselves why we have defined DK-equivalences exclusively on dg-Segal spaces, and not on any functor. The reason is that if we do that, DK-equivalences would not satisfy the two-out-of-three condition. Indeed, if we have three morphisms
f
,
g
and
f\circ g
, and two out of the three are DK-equivalences over any functor, then the third has the second condition of DK-equivalences because of the two-out-of-three condition on quasi-equivalences; but the first condition is not always true. It is only true if we have
f,g
DK-equivalences or
g
and
f\circ g
DK-equivalences. It is true if the spaces are all dg-Segal, though.

Remark 4.3.21
.

If
F,G
is of the form
F=\Sing(T),G=\Sing(T^{\prime})
, what we just defined is pretty much exactly a weak equivalence between dg-categories.

Now, the rest of this result hasn’t been finished yet. We will discuss it in more detail in the next chapter, but for now, here is it.

Hypothesis 4.3.22
.

Let
f:F\to G
be a morphism between two functors satisfying the dg-Segal conditions. Then
f
is a DK-equivalence if and only if it is a weak equivalence in the complete dg-Segal model structure.

We have now everything we need in order to prove that the functor is fully faithful.

4.4
Hypercovers and free dg-categories

The next step of our proof is proving that
\Sing
is, in fact, fully faithful. To do that, we will prove that the restriction to
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
injects fully-faithfully, and then go on to prove that we can write every dg-category as a particular colimit of objects in
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
and that
\Sing_{W}
commutes with those colimits. And in order to do that, we will use hypercovers.

Hypercovers are a useful concept, and it has been defined in several different contexts. Some traits remain, though: in all of them, a hypercover of an object
A
is some kind of augmented object
U_{*}\to A
with a similar set of conditions, and in most of them we have the property that
\hocolim U_{n}\simeq A
. For example, Dugger and Isaksen proved in
[
DI04
]
that we do have that weak equivalence
\hocolim U_{n}\simeq A
result in
\mathbf{Top}
. In our case, the classic notion of hypercover that we will use more often is the one in the category of simplicial sets,
\mathbf{sSet}
. For that, we will give here its definition. Although any reader familiar with Lurie’s work will know that the definition there is given in much greater generality, we have decided to translate it here to the language of simplicial objects because it is the only context in which we will use it.

Definition 4.4.1
.

[
Lur09
, Definition 6.5.3.2, Corollary 7.2.1.15]
Let
X\in\mathbf{sSet}
be a simplicial set, and
U_{*}\to X
an augmented simplicial object in
\mathbf{sSet}
. We say that
U_{*}
is
a hypercover of
X
if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
the functor

U_{n}\to U_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}}

is an effective epimorphism. In other words,
U_{*}\to X
is a hypercover if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}

\pi_{0}(U_{n})\to\pi_{0}(U_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}})

is an epimorphism.

Proposition 4.4.2
.

[
Lur09
, Theorem 6.5.3.12]
Let
X\in\mathbf{sSet}
be a simplicial set and
U_{*}\to X
a hypercover of simplicial sets. Then we have that
\hocolim U_{n}\simeq X
.

Unluckily for us, none of the known definitions of hypercover work for the context we want, so we’ll be forced to write our own.

Definition 4.4.3
.

Let
M
be a model category and
M_{0}
be a subcategory of
M
. Then we define
f:T\to T^{\prime}
a morphism in
M
to be
an
M_{0}
-epimorphism
if for all
X\in M_{0}
the induced functor

\Map(X,T)\to\Map(X,T^{\prime})

is an effective epimorphism in
\mathbf{sSet}
, i.e. the morphism

\pi_{0}(\Map(X,T))\to\pi_{0}(\Map(X,T^{\prime}))

is surjective.

Definition 4.4.4
.

Let
M
be a model category and
M_{0}
be a subcategory of
M
. Let
X\in M
be an object of
M
, and
U_{*}\to X
an augmented simplicial object in
M
. We say that
U_{*}
is
an
M_{0}
-hypercover of
X
if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
the functor

U_{n}\to U_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}}

is an
M_{0}
-epimorphism. In other words, using Proposition
3.3.16
,
U_{*}\to X
is an
M_{0}
-hypercover if

U_{0}\to X

is an
M_{0}
-epimorphism and for all
n\geq 1

U_{n}\to(\mathbb{R}\cosk_{n-1}\sk_{n-1}U)_{n}

is an
M_{0}
-epimorphism.

Now that we have a definition of an
M_{0}
-hypercover, the next step will be proving that for all elements of a category we can construct a hypercover made entirely of objects in
M_{0}
. Of course, this cannot work for a general
M_{0}
: we will need a few extra conditions. As usual in these cases, we will construct our hypercover level by level, so we need a definition of a
n
-truncated hypercover.

Definition 4.4.5
.

Let
M
be a model category and
M_{0}
be a subcategory of
M
. Let
X\in M
be an object in
M
. We define
an
n
-truncated
M_{0}
-hypercover of
X
to be an augmented
n
-truncated simplicial set
X_{*}\to X
where for all
i\leq n

X_{i}\to X_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{i}}

is an
M_{0}
-epimorphism.

And now for the theorem:

Theorem 4.4.6
.

Let
M
be a model category where every object is fibrant, and let
M_{0}
be a subcategory of
M
which is closed for finite coproducts. We assume that for every
X\in M
there exists an object in
M_{0}
,
U\in M_{0}
, and a morphism
U\to X
that is an
M_{0}
-epimorphism. Then there exists an
M_{0}
-hypercover of
X
consisting of objects in
M_{0}
.

Proof.

We are going to prove this by induction. Let
X\in M
be an object in
M
. We will prove that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an
n
-truncated hypercover of
X
.

\bullet\ \ n=0
. This is true by hypothesis. We have assumed that there exists an object
U_{0}\in M_{0}
with a morphism
U_{0}\to X
which is
M_{0}
-epi. We then take the
0
-truncated simplicial set which is
U_{0}
on degree
0
. It is a
0
-truncated hypercover by definition.

\bullet\ \ n\in\mathbb{N}
. By induction hypothesis there exists an
n
-truncated hypercover of
X
, named
V_{*}\to X
. We have to construct an
(n+1)
-truncated hypercover of
X
, that we will call
U_{*}\to X
.

We define
V_{*}^{\prime}=\sk_{n+1}(\cosk_{n}V_{*})
. This simplicial object is
(n+1)
-truncated, but the term
n+1
is not necessarily in
M_{0}
. By hypothesis, there exists a morphism
U^{\prime}_{n+1}\to V_{n+1}^{\prime}
that is a
M_{0}
-epi and such that
U^{\prime}_{n+1}\in M_{0}
. We define the
(n+1)
-truncated
M_{0}
-hypercover
U_{*}\to X
to be
U_{i}=V_{i}^{\prime}=V_{i}
for all
i\leq n
and

U_{n+1}=U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod_{i\leq n}V_{i}

for
n+1
, where we take the colimit over
i\leq n
. As the set of elements
i\leq n
is finite and we have assumed that
M_{0}
is closed for finite colimits, we still have that
U_{n+1}\in M_{0}
.

We need to prove that
U_{*}\to X
is an augmented simplicial set, and also that we have the
M_{0}
-hypercover property. Let us construct the morphisms
U_{m}\to U_{n+1}
and
U_{n+1}\to U_{m}
for all morphism
[n+1]\to[m]
and
[m]\to[n+1]
, with
m\leq n+1
.

These morphisms are straightforward: in one direction we just take the composite

U_{n+1}\to U^{\prime}_{n+1}\to V^{\prime}_{n+1}\to V^{\prime}_{m}=U_{m}.

and in the other sense we just take the morphism given by the colimit.

And lastly, we need to prove that for all
i\leq n+1
the morphism
U_{i}\to U_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{i}}
is a
M_{0}
-epimorphism.

For
i\leq n
this is true by induction hypothesis. Indeed, we have defined
U_{*}
in such a way that
U_{i}=V^{\prime}_{i}
, and by Remark
3.3.12
we have that
V^{\prime}_{i}=(\sk_{n+1}(\cosk_{n}V_{*}))_{i}=V_{i}
for all
i\leq n
. We need then to prove that

U_{i}=V^{\prime}_{i}=V_{i}\to(\cosk_{i-1}\sk_{i-1}U_{*})_{i}=(\cosk_{i-1}\sk_{i-1}V_{*})_{i}

is an
M_{0}
-epimorphism. Since
V_{*}
is an
n
-truncated
M_{0}
-hypercover, this condition is verified. We only need to prove this for
n+1
.

We need to prove, then, that the morphism

U_{n+1}=U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i}\to(U_{*})^{\partial\Delta^{n}}=(\cosk_{n}\sk_{n}U_{*})_{n+1}=(\cosk_{n}V_{*})_{n+1}=V^{\prime}_{n+1}

is an
M_{0}
-epimorphism. Let
W\in M_{0}
be an object in
M_{0}
, we are going to prove that

\Map(W,U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i})\to\Map(W,V^{\prime}_{n+1})

is surjective on the
\pi_{0}
. Every object in
M
is fibrant by hypothesis, which means that we can write
\Map(W,U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i})
as
\Hom(C^{*}(W),U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i})
, and similarly for
\Map(W,V^{\prime}_{n+1})
. We are, then, going to prove that

\pi_{0}(\Map(W,U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i}))=\Hom(W,U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i})/\sim\to\pi_{0}(\Map(W,V^{\prime}_{n+1}))=\Hom(W,V^{\prime}_{n+1})/\sim

is surjective, where
\sim
is the homotopy equivalence relation.

We will need an auxiliary morphism for this. By definition of a simplicial set, for all
i\leq n
there exists a morphism
V_{i}=V^{\prime}_{i}\to V^{\prime}_{n+1}
. Also, by the way we have constructed
U_{*}
, there exists a morphism
u:U^{\prime}_{n+1}\to V^{\prime}_{n+1}
, which is an
M_{0}
-epimorphism. In consequence, by definition of a coproduct, there exists a morphism
f:U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i}\to V^{\prime}_{n+1}
, and a factorization
g:U^{\prime}_{n+1}\to U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i}
such that
f\circ g=u
.

And now let us prove the surjectivity. Let
F:W\to V^{\prime}_{n+1}
be a morphism. As
u:U^{\prime}_{n+1}\to V^{\prime}_{n+1}
is an
M_{0}
-epimorphism, that means that it exists, up to homotopy, a morphism
F^{\prime}:W\to U^{\prime}_{n+1}
such that
u\circ F^{\prime}=F
. But we have said that
u
factorizes through
f
. We can then, compute a morphism

g\circ F^{\prime}:W\to U^{\prime}_{n+1}\to U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i}

such that
f\circ g\circ F^{\prime}=u\circ F^{\prime}=F
up to homotopy. We have that the morphism

\pi_{0}(\Map(W,U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i}))=\Hom(W,U^{\prime}_{n+1}\coprod V_{i})/\sim\to\pi_{0}(\Map(W,V^{\prime}_{n+1}))=\Hom(W,V^{\prime}_{n+1})/\sim

is an
M_{0}
-epimorphism. The
(n+1)
-truncated simplicial set
U_{*}\to X
is an
(n+1)
-truncated
M_{0}
-hypercover of
X
and we have finished the proof.

∎

As the reader can imagine, we decided to use the free dg-categories of finite type expecting them to be well-behaved enough that we can work with
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercovers. But even though it would be possible to construct
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercovers directly, the number of objects in it would explode quite quickly, and we don’t want that. So instead of working on the free dg-categories of finite type directly, we will first fix the objects. Once that is done, it will be the moment to make good on our word: let us prove that that is actually true.

Proposition 4.4.7
.

Let
X
be a dg-category, and let
\mathcal{O}
be its set of objects. Let
M
be
\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}
the model category of dg-categories with
\mathcal{O}
as a set of objects, and
M_{0}=c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{O}}
be the full subcategory of free dg-categories of finite type with
\mathcal{O}
as a set of objects. There exists an
M_{0}
-hypercover
U_{*}\to X
such that
U_{i}\in M_{0}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We just need to prove that the model category
M
and the subcategory
M_{0}
fulfill the conditions of Theorem
4.4.6
, i.e. that all object in
M
is fibrant, that
M_{0}
is closed for finite coproducts and that for all object
X\in M
there exists an object
U\in M_{0}
and a
M_{0}
-epimorphism
U\to X
.

—

The first condition is Corollary
3.4.10
.

—

Let
X,Y
be two free dg-categories over
\mathcal{O}
. Then, by definition, there exists two graphs
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in Gr(\mathbf{C(k)})^{tf}
such that
L(X^{\prime})=X
and
L(Y^{\prime})=Y
. But
L
is a left adjoint, and a finite coproduct is a special case of a colimit: as such, we know that
L
commutes with finite coproducts. That gives us that
X\coprod Y=L(X^{\prime})\coprod L(Y^{\prime})=L(X^{\prime}\coprod Y^{\prime})
, and we have that
X\coprod Y
is a free category of finite type, coming from the coproduct of graphs
X^{\prime}\coprod Y^{\prime}
. We have that
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
is closed for finite coproducts and we have finished.

—

Let
X
be a dg-category. We are going to prove that the morphism
LU(X)\to X
is the
M_{0}
-epimorphism we need. First of all, by definition of a free dg-category,
LU(X)
has the same objects as
X
and it is an object in
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{O}}
. Let
A
be a free dg-category of finite type over
\mathcal{O}
. We need to prove, then, that

\Map(A,LU(X))\to\Map(A,X)

is surjective on the
\pi_{0}
. But this morphism is already surjective. Indeed, if we take
f:C^{*}(A)\to X
, we can define a morphism
f^{\prime}:C^{*}(A)\to LU(X)
that gives us the right result. Indeed,
X
and
LU(X)
have the same objects, so that won’t change. We only need to define the morphisms of complexes.

Let
x,y\in C^{*}(A)
. Then we have a morphism
\phi:C^{*}(A)(x,y)\to X(f(x),f(y))
. Now, we remind the reader that, by definition
3.4.16
, the morphisms in
L(U(X))
are defined as follows:

L(U(X))(f(x),f(y))=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\in\mathcal{O}}(U(X)(f(x),f(x_{1}))\otimes\ldots\otimes U(X)(f(x_{m}),f(y))).

We define the morphism
\phi^{\prime}:C^{*}(A)(x,y)\to LU(X)(f(x),f(y))
such that for all
g\in C^{*}(A)(x,y)
, the image by
\phi^{\prime}
is the direct sum with
\phi(g)
in the zero component and zero everywhere else.

We have all three conditions for the existence of an
M_{0}
-hypercover. For all
X
there exists a
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercover of
X
composed of objects in
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
.
∎

Remark 4.4.8
.

There is one important thing that we need to highlight from this construction. If we have a
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercover
U_{*}\to X
defined like that then for all
x,y\in\Ob(X)
and for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have that
U_{n}(x,y)\to U_{*}(x,y)^{\partial\Delta^{n}}
is a split epimorphism.

This second remark gives us a concept we will need for the next result.

Definition 4.4.9
.

We define a
split hypercover
of
E
in
\mathbf{C(k)}
to be an augmented simplicial complex
E_{*}\to E
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
the morphism
E_{n}\to E_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}}
is a split epimorphism.

Once this definition is set, there is also another thing we have to keep in mind.

Corollary 4.4.10
.

If we have an augmented simplicial object
T_{*}\to T
in
\mathbf{dg-cat}_{O}
such that for all
x,y\in\mathcal{O}
the associated augmented simplicial complex
T_{*}(x,y)\to T(x,y)
is a split hypercover in
\mathbf{C(k)}
, then
T_{*}\to T
is a hypercover in
\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

We have now a definition of hypercover on dg-categories. When we described them at first, we gave one important property that those constructions tended to have: their homotopy colimits being quasi-equivalent to the original object. And we would like for it to happen in this context too. Let us work on that. In order to do so, though, we will need an auxiliary result beforehand.

Lemma 4.4.11
.

Let
M=\mathbf{dg-cat}
and
M_{0}=c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
. Let
T
be an object in
\mathbf{dg-cat}
and
T_{*}\to T
a
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercover constructed using Theorem
4.4.6
and Proposition
4.4.7
. Then for all
x,y\in\Ob(T)
we have that
\hocolim_{\Delta^{op}}(T_{i}(x,y))\simeq T(x,y)
in
\mathbf{C(k)}
.

Proof.

As we said in the last remark, if
T_{*}\to T
is a
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercover constructed using the aforementioned theorem, then for all
x,y\in\mathcal{O}
,
T_{*}(x,y)\to T(x,y)
is a split epimorphism. We will then prove that if
E_{*}\to E
is a split hypercover of complexes, then
\hocolim_{\Delta^{op}}E_{i}\simeq E
.

We start with the connective case. Let us assume that
E_{*},E\in\mathbf{C(k)}^{\leq 0}
, and
E_{*}\to E
is a split hypercover in
\mathbf{C(k)}^{\leq 0}
. We can use the Dold-Kan equivalence,
DK:\mathbf{sSet}\rightleftharpoons\mathbf{C(k)}^{\leq 0}:DK^{-1}
, and
DK^{-1}(E_{*})\to DK^{-1}(E)
is surjective over
\pi_{0}
in
\mathbf{sSet}
, i.e. it is a hypercover of simplicial sets. By Proposition
4.4.2
we have that
\hocolim_{\Delta^{op}}DK^{-1}(E_{i})\simeq DK^{-1}(E)
in
\mathbf{sSet}
. But the Dold-Kan equivalence isn’t just an equivalence: it is a model equivalence, meaning that it is an equivalence of categories that also preserves the model structure (
[
GJ09
, Chapter III, Section 2]
). In particular, if we apply
DK
we have that
\hocolim_{\Delta^{op}}E_{i}\simeq E
and we have finished.

We have then that the connective case is true. We will now reduce the general case to the connective case, using the naïve truncation
\tau:\mathbf{C(k)}\to\mathbf{C(k)}^{\leq 0}
such that if
E\in\mathbf{C(k)}
,
H^{i}(\tau(E))=H^{i}(E)
for all
i\leq 0
and
H^{i}(\tau(E))=0
for all
i>0
. But knowing that
E_{*}\to E
is a split hypercover in
\mathbf{C(k)}
doesn’t assure us that
\tau(E_{*})\to\tau(E)
will also be a split hypercover in
\mathbf{C(k)}^{\leq 0}
. To prove that, we need the following result.

Sub-lemma 4.4.12
.

Let
E_{*}\to E
be a split hypercover in
\mathbf{C(k)}
and
K
a finite simplicial object. Then we have the equivalence

H^{i}(E_{*}^{K})\simeq H^{i}(E_{*})^{K}.

Proof.

We are going to prove this by induction over the dimension of
K
. Let us prove that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\dim K=n
, then
H^{i}(E_{*}^{K})\simeq H^{i}(E_{*})^{K}
.

—

n=0
. This is almost immediate. Indeed, if
\dim K=0
, then
K=\coprod_{p}*
, and
E_{*}^{K}=E_{0}^{p}
. And as cohomology commutes with products, we have that
H^{i}(E_{*}^{K})\simeq H^{i}(E_{*})^{K}
.

—

n\geq 1
. We assume that for every
K^{\prime}
with
\dim K^{\prime}<n
we have
H^{i}(E_{*}^{K^{\prime}})\simeq H^{i}(E_{*})^{K^{\prime}}
. Let
K
be a simplicial objet of dimension
n
. Then we have the following homotopy coproduct
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod\partial\Delta^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod\Delta^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\leq n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner^{h}}

where
K_{\leq n-1}
is the simplicial subobject of
K
of dimension
n-1
. In turn, that square gives us the following homotopy products
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{*}^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{*}^{K_{\leq n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod E_{*}^{\Delta^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod E_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner^{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(E_{*})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(E_{*})^{K_{\leq n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod H^{i}(E_{*})^{\Delta^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod H^{i}(E_{*})^{\partial\Delta^{n}}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner^{h}}

Now, we would like to have that if we take the
H^{i}
on the first square we still have a homotopic product. That is not true in general. But we know something extra about this square: indeed, as
E_{*}\to E
is a split hypercover, we know that
\prod E_{*}^{\Delta^{n}}\to\prod E_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}}
is a split epimorphism, and in that case we do have that the square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(E_{*}^{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(E_{*}^{K_{\leq n-1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod H^{i}(E_{*}^{\Delta^{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod H^{i}(E_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner^{h}}

is a homotopic product. By induction, we have
H^{i}(E_{*})^{K_{\leq n-1}}\simeq H^{i}(E_{*}^{K_{\leq n-1})}
and
\prod H^{i}(E_{*})^{\partial\Delta^{n}}\simeq\prod H^{i}(E_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}})
(because
\dim K_{\leq n-1}=\dim\partial\Delta^{n}=n-1
); and by definition we have that
H^{i}(E_{*})^{\Delta^{n}}\simeq H^{i}(E_{n})\simeq H^{i}(E_{*}^{\Delta^{n}})
. We have then the following cubic diagram, where both the front and the back square are homotopic products and three out of four front-to-back arrows are equivalences.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(E_{*}^{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(E_{*}^{K_{\leq n-1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(E_{*})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(E_{*})^{K_{\leq n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod H^{i}(E_{*}^{\Delta^{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod H^{i}(E_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod H^{i}(E_{*})^{\Delta^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod H^{i}(E_{*})^{\partial\Delta^{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

We have that the fourth arrow is also an equivalence,
H^{i}(E_{*}^{K})\simeq H^{i}(E_{*})^{K}
, and we have finished.

∎

Now that we have that, we can prove that if
E_{*}\to E
is a split hypercover, then
\tau(E_{*})\to\tau(E)
is too. Indeed, by Sublemma
4.4.12
, we have that for all
i\leq 0
,

H^{i}(\tau(E_{*})^{\partial\Delta^{n}})\simeq H^{i}(\tau(E_{*}))^{\partial\Delta^{n}}\simeq H^{i}(E_{*})^{\partial\Delta^{n}}\simeq H^{i}(\tau(E_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}}))

and
\tau(E_{*})^{\partial\Delta^{n}}\simeq\tau(E_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}})
. We then have that for all
n\in\mathbb{N},

\tau(E)_{n}\to\tau(E_{*})^{\partial\Delta^{n}}\simeq\tau(E_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}})
is a split epimorphism, and
\tau(E_{*})\to\tau(E)
is a split epimorphism.

Now, if
E_{*}\to E
is a split hypercover, we have that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
E_{*}[-i]\to E[-i]
is also a split hypercover and
\tau(E_{*}[-i])\to\tau(E[-i])
is too. By the connective case, we have that
\hocolim\tau(E_{n}[-i])\simeq\tau(E[-i])
. As this is true for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that
\hocolim E_{n}\simeq E
.

In conclusion, if
T_{*}\to T
is a hypercover constructed using Theorem
4.4.6
, we have that
\hocolim(T_{i}(x,y))\simeq T(x,y)
for all
x,y\in\Ob(T)
in
\mathbf{C(k)}
and we have finished.

∎

We’re almost ready to prove that if
T_{*}\to T
is a
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercover constructed as instructed, then
\hocolim T_{i}\to T
is a weak equivalence. We have proven that the complexes of morphisms have that condition. But does it transfer well from complexes to dg-categories and vice-versa?

Lemma 4.4.13
.

The forgetful functor
U:\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}\to Gr(\mathbf{C(k)})_{\mathcal{O}}
commutes with homotopy colimits over
\Delta^{op}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
[
Lur17
, Lemma 4.1.8.13.]
, in particular of its proof. Indeed, in that result we say that if we have a combinatorial monoidal model category
A
and a small category
C
such that its nerve
N(C)
is sifted, and we have on one hand that
A
’s monoidal structure is symmetric and satisfies the monoid axiom, and on the other hand that
A
is left proper and its cofibrations are generated by cofibrations between cofibrant objects; then, the forgetful functor
\Alg(A)\to A
commutes with homotopy colimits over
C
.

Now, we take the monoidal structure on the graphs with fixed objects to be as follows: let
G,G^{\prime}\in Gr(\mathbf{C(k)})_{\mathcal{O}}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{O}
, we define

(G\otimes G^{\prime})(x,y)=\oplus_{z}G(x,z)\otimes G^{\prime}(z,y).

The category of dg-categories with fixed objects is the category of algebras over
Gr(\mathbf{C(k)})_{\mathcal{O}}
with this monoidal structure,
\Alg(Gr(\mathbf{C(k)})_{\mathcal{O}})
. As the category of graphs with this monoidal structure satisfies the above conditions and
N(\Delta)
is sifted, we have that the forgetful functor
\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}\to Gr(\mathbf{C(k)})_{\mathcal{O}}
commutes with homotopy colimits and we have finished our proof.

∎

And now we’re ready to prove our result.

Proposition 4.4.14
.

Let
T
be a dg-category with fixed objects, and
T_{*}\to T
a
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercover in
\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}
, the category of dg-categories with fixed objects
\mathcal{O}=\Ob(T)
. Then we have that
\hocolim T_{i}\simeq T
in
\mathbf{dg-cat}
.

Proof.

Let
\phi:\hocolim T_{*}\to T
be the morphism from the homotopy colimit to
T
. We need to prove that this morphism is a weak equivalence in
\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}
. But as we have the same objects, the quasi-essential surjectivity is automatic. We only need to prove that
\phi
is quasi-fully faithful. By definition, that means that for all
x,y\in\mathcal{O}
, we need to prove that
(\hocolim T_{i})(x,y)\simeq T(x,y)
. But as we know from Lemma
4.4.13
that the forgetful functor commutes with
\hocolim
, that is equivalent to asking that for all
x,y\in\mathcal{O}
, we have
\hocolim(T_{i}(x,y))\simeq T(x,y)
. By Lemma
4.4.11
, that is true.

We have that
\phi:\hocolim T_{i}\simeq T
in
\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}
. If we can prove that the functor
\Phi:\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}\to\mathbf{dg-cat}
commutes with homotopy colimits over
\Delta^{op}
, we have finished the proof. Let
X=\hocolim_{\Delta^{op}}T_{i}
in
\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}
, and let us call the forgetful morphisms
\Phi:\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}\to\mathbf{dg-cat}
and
\Xi:\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}\to\coprod_{\mathcal{O}}k/\mathbf{dg-cat}
, where for all
X\in\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}

\Xi(X)
gives us a morphism from
\coprod_{\mathcal{O}}k
to
\Phi(X)
.

For all
T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{dg-cat}
we then have a morphism of the form

\Map(\Phi(X),T^{\prime})\to\Map(\coprod k,T^{\prime})\simeq\prod\Map(k,T^{\prime}).

By getting the fiber of this morphism and doing the same thing with
\hocolim T_{i}
, we get the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(X,T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(\Xi(X),T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(\Phi(X),T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\Map(k,T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\holim\Map(T_{i},T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\holim\Map(\Xi(T_{i}),T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\holim\Map(\Phi(T_{i}),T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\Map(k,T^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

If we can prove that
f
is a weak equivalence, we have finished. For that, we are going to use the far-left square of this diagram. Indeed, if the morphisms
\Map(\Xi,T^{\prime})
and
\holim\Map(\Xi,T^{\prime})
are weak equivalences, by 2-out-of-3 then
f
will be a weak equivalence too. If we can prove that
\Xi
is fully faithful, we will have everything we need.

We have an adjunction
\Xi:\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightleftharpoons\coprod k/\mathbf{dg-cat}:\Gamma
where the right adjoint
\Gamma
is such that for all
(K,\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{O}},x_{\alpha}\in\Ob(K))\in\coprod k/\mathbf{dg-cat}
, the objects of
\Gamma(K)
are
\mathcal{O}
and for every
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{O}
,
\Gamma(K)(\alpha,\beta)=K(x_{\alpha},x_{\beta})
. It is easy to see that
\Gamma\Xi=\Id_{\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}}
. We have then that
\Xi
is fully faithful and then that
\Map(\Xi,T^{\prime})
is a weak equivalence.

As such, we have that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(X,T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(\Xi(X),T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\holim\Map(T_{i},T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\holim\Map(\Xi(T_{i}),T^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

and
f
is a weak equivalence. So
\Phi
commutes with colimits, and since
\hocolim T_{i}\simeq T
in
\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we have
\hocolim T_{i}\simeq T
in
\mathbf{dg-cat}
and we have finished this proof.

∎

We can now try to prove the full faithfullness of our adjoint functor
\Sing
.

4.5
The functor
Sing
is fully faithful

Let us prove that
\Sing
is, in fact, fully faithful. To do that, we will prove that the restriction to
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
injects fully-faithfully, and then go on to prove that we can write every dg-category as a certain colimit of elements in
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
and that
\Sing
commutes with those colimits. The first part is fairly obvious.

Proposition 4.5.1
.

With the construction from Theorem
4.1.4
, the functor

\Sing^{c\mathcal{L}}:\Ho(c\mathcal{L})\to\Ho(\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet}))

is fully faithful.

Proof.

For this, we use Theorems
3.3.50
and
3.3.53
, taking the model category to be
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
and
W
to be the weak equivalences in the full subcategory of
\mathbf{dg-cat}
of free categories of finite type. Indeed, if we go down to the homotopy category, we can factorize
\Sing^{c\mathcal{L}}
as

\Ho(c\mathcal{L})\to\Ho(\mathbf{sSet}^{c\mathcal{L},W})\to\Ho(\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(L_{W}c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet}))=\Ho(\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{op},\mathbf{sSet})).

But we know from Theorem
3.3.53
that the first morphism here is fully faithful, and from Theorem
3.3.50
that the second one is an equivalence. So the composition of the two is fully faithful, and we have finished our proof.

∎

We have finally everything needed in order to use our free hypercovers. Let us prove that we have the necessary DK-equivalences.

Lemma 4.5.2
.

Let
T_{*}\to T
be a
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercover of
T
constructed as in Theorem
4.4.6
and Proposition
4.4.7
, with
T\in\mathbf{dg-cat}
a dg-category. Then the homotopy colimit of its image by
\Sing_{k}
,
\hocolim(\Sing_{k}(T_{i}))
, satisfies the dg-Segal conditions.

Proof.

In order to prove this result, we need to prove that the homotopy colimit fulfills the three conditions of the definition of dg-Segal space. The first two are easy, and hinge on the fact that the homotopy colimit in this case commutes with finite products and all images of
\Sing
satisfy the dg-Segal conditions.

1. Let
L,K
be two free dg-categories of finite type. By the first dg-Segal condition on the image of
\Sing
,

\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))(K\coprod L)=\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i})(K\coprod L))\simeq\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i})(K)\times\Sing(T_{i})(L)).

And as the homotopy colimits over
\Delta^{op}
commute with finite products,

\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))(K\coprod L)\simeq\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i})(K))\times\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i})(L))

and we have finished.

2. The second property is even easier. Indeed,

\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))(\emptyset)\simeq\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i})(\emptyset))\simeq\hocolim(*)\simeq*.

We arrive now to the third condition. For an issue of generality and also in order to lighten our notation, we will do the computations in a slightly larger context, with the help of this sublemma.

Sub-lemma 4.5.3
.

Let
F\in\Fun^{\mathbb{S}}(c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{c,op},\mathbf{sSet})
be a functor that satisfies conditions 1 and 2 of the dg-Segal conditions, and also an additional condition as follows:

4.

For all
L,K\in c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}^{c}
,
F(L\coprod_{k\coprod k}K)\to F(L)\times_{F(k)\times F(k)}F(K)
is a weak equivalence. In other words,
F
transforms coproducts into products.

Then we have that
F
satisfies condition 3 of the dg-Segal conditions. In other words, for all
G\in Gr(\mathbf{C(k)})
a graph of finite type,
x,y\in\Ob(G)
two objects in
G
, and
\alpha\in Z^{n}(G(x,y))
a cycle in
G(x,y)
, the following diagram is a homotopy pullback
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(L(G(<\alpha>)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(L(G))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,n,1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\Delta_{k}(1,n,1)).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner^{h}}

Proof.

In order to make this proof easier to follow, we start with the case where
G
has two objects and move up from there. Assume
G
is a free dg-category with two objects. There is a morphism
\gamma:F(G)\to\Map(\mathcal{O}_{G},\mathcal{O})
, where
\mathcal{O}_{G}=\Ob(G)
is the set of objects of
G
and
\mathcal{O}=\pi_{0}(F(k))
. We know that
\pi_{0}(\Map(\mathcal{O}_{G},\mathcal{O}))
is the set of morphisms from
\mathcal{O}_{G}
to
\mathcal{O}
. Then, if we take
f:\mathcal{O}_{G}\to\mathcal{O}
, the fiber of
\gamma
is
F_{(f(x),f(y))}(G)
. By conflating the two points on
G
with the two points on
\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,s,1)
and
\Delta_{k}(1,s,1)
, we can reduce the problem to asking that for all
f:\mathcal{O}_{G}\to\mathcal{O}
the following diagram is a homotopy pullback
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{(f(x),f(y))}(G(<\alpha>))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{(f(x),f(y))}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{(f(x),f(y))}(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,n,1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{(f(x),f(y))}(\Delta_{k}(1,n,1))}

But we already know that for all dg-Segal space
F
,
F_{(f(x),f(y))}(E_{x,y})\simeq\Map(E,F(f(x),f(y)))
, where
E_{x,y}
is the free category given by two points and
E
as the morphism of complexes from
x
to
y
. So the question ends up being whether
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(G<\alpha>,F(f(x),f(y)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(G,F(f(x),f(y)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(k^{c}[n],F(f(x),f(y)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(k[n],F(f(x),f(y)))}

is a homotopy pullback. But this diagram is in
\mathbf{sSet}
, which is a proper model category, meaning that if one of the morphisms of this diagram is a fibration, we have finished. And indeed, the morphism
k[n]\to k^{c}[n]
is a generating cofibration of
\mathbf{C(k)}
, which means that the morphism
\Map(k^{c}[n],F(f(x),f(y)))\to\Map(k[n],F(f(x),f(y)))
is a fibration. The diagram is a homotopy pullback and we have finished. If
G
has two objects,
F
satisfies the third dg-Segal condition on it.

For the passage from two elements to more, it is a question of noticing that condition 3 is merely a local condition: intuitively, the only change being made in it is in relation to the morphisms between
x
and
y
. Outside of that, it changes nothing whether the original free dg-category
G
had two objects or three thousand. Following that logic, we will decompose
G
in two sections: one that changes and one that does not.

Let
G
be a free dg-category of finite type, and let
x,y
be two objects in
G
. It is easy to see that we can write it as
G=G^{0}\coprod_{k\coprod k}G_{x,y}
, where
G_{x,y}
is a free dg-category with two objects and
G(x,y)
as the morphism complex between those objects; and
G^{0}
is a free dg-category such that
G^{0}
has the same objects as
G
and
G^{0}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=G(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
if
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\neq(x,y)
and
G^{0}(x,y)=0
. As
G_{x,y}
is a free dg-category with two objects, we have that
F
satisfies the third dg-Segal condition on
G_{x,y}
. We can then construct a tower of homotopy pullbacks of the following form:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(G^{0})\times_{F(k)\times F(k)}F(G_{x,y}(<\alpha>))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(G^{0})\times_{F(k)\times F(k)}F(G_{x,y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(G_{x,y}(<\alpha>))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(G_{x,y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\Delta_{k}^{c}(1,n,1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\Delta_{k}(1,n,1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner^{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner^{h}}

and the outside square is a homotopy pullback. By condition 4, we have that

F(G^{0})\times_{F(k)\times F(k)}F(G_{x,y}(<\alpha>))\simeq F(G^{0}\coprod_{k\coprod k}G_{x,y}<\alpha>)\simeq F(G(<\alpha>)

and

F(G^{0})\times_{F(k)\times F(k)}F(G_{x,y})\simeq F(G^{0}\coprod_{k\coprod k}G_{x,y})\simeq F(G),

so we have our condition 3 for all
G
free dg-categories of finite type and we have finished our proof.

∎

We now want to apply this sublemma to our homotopy colimit. We have already proven that
\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))
satisfies conditions 1 and 2 of the dg-Segal conditions; we just need to prove that it also satisfies condition 4 of the sublemma. Let
K,L
be two free dg-categories of finite type. In a similar way than the proof of Proposition
4.2.5
, we have that

\Sing(K\coprod_{k\coprod k}L)\simeq\Sing(K)\times_{\Sing(k)^{2}}\Sing(L).

But as long as the base of the finite product has a finite
\pi_{0}
, we have that the homotopy colimit commutes with it. Which means that

\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))(K\coprod_{k\coprod k}L)\simeq\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i})(K)\times_{\Sing(k)^{2}}\Sing(T_{i})(L))\simeq

\simeq\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i})(K))\times_{\hocolim(\Sing(k))^{2}}\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i})(L)).

Hence the functor
\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))
satisfies the condition 4 of the sublemma, and in consequence it satisfies the third condition of a dg-Segal space.

The functor
\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))
satisfies all three dg-Segal conditions, and we have finished our proof.

∎

Remark 4.5.4
.

We have proven here that if we have the first two dg-Segal conditions and also that
F(L\coprod_{k\coprod k}K)\to F(L)\times_{F(k)\times F(k)}F(K)
, we have a dg-Segal space; and also we proved in Proposition
4.2.11
that if we have a dg-Segal space, then at the very least
F(\Delta_{k}^{n})\simeq F(\Delta_{k}^{1})\times_{F(k)}\ldots\times_{F(k)}F(\Delta_{k}^{1})
. This is starting to resemble strongly the definition of a Segal space. Indeed, we think it is probable that we could rewrite the definition of dg-Segal spaces to reflect way more closely that definition. We have decided not to do so because we think this definition emphasizes better the linear structure. In any case, this is not a minimal construction anyway: see Chapter 3 for more details.

Now that we know that
\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))
is a dg-Segal space, we can see whether the induced morphism
\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))\to\Sing(T)
is a DK-equivalence in
\mathbf{dg-Segal}
.

Lemma 4.5.5
.

Let
T_{*}\to T
be a
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercover of
T
constructed as in Theorem
4.4.6
and Proposition
4.4.7
. Then the morphism
\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))\to\Sing(T)
is a DK-equivalence.

Proof.

In order to prove this, we need to make sure this morphism satisfies both conditions of the definition. The first is easy: indeed, we have made sure by the construction of our hypercover that all terms of the hypercover have the same elements. So
\left[\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))\right]\to\left[\Sing(T)\right]
is essentially surjective.

As for the condition on the morphisms, we have done most of the work already. We know that for all
(x,y)\in\pi_{0}(\Sing(T)(k))
,
\Sing(T)_{(x,y)}(E)=\Map(E,T(x,y))
. So we can rewrite this condition as being whether

\hocolim(\Map(-,T_{i}(x,y))\simeq\Map(-,T(x,y)).

But we know that
T_{*}\to T
is a
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercover, which in particular means that for all
x,y\in T
,
T_{n}(x,y)\to T_{*}(x,y)^{\partial\Delta^{n}}
is a split epimorphism. In consequence, we have that for all
E\in\mathbf{C(k)}
,

\pi_{0}(\Map(E,T_{n}(x,y)))\to\pi_{0}(\Map(E,T_{*}(x,y)^{\partial\Delta^{n}}))\simeq\pi_{0}(\Map(E,T_{*}(x,y))^{\partial\Delta^{n}})

is an epimorphism. But we have seen that this is exactly the definition of a hypercover in
\mathbf{sSet}
. So we have that
\Map(E,T_{*}(x,y))
is a hypercover of
\Map(E,T(x,y))
in the simplicial sets, and by Proposition
4.4.2
that means that for all
E
we have
\hocolim\Map(E,T_{*}(x,y))\simeq\Map(E,T(x,y))
. That is, by definition, the same as saying that

\hocolim(\Map(-,T_{i}(x,y))\to\Map(-,T(x,y))

is a weak equivalence.

The second condition is fulfilled and the morphism
\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))\to\Sing(T)
is a DK-equivalence.

∎

Finally, we have enough information to prove the full faithfullness of our functor
\Sing
.

Theorem 4.5.6
.

Assuming Hypothesis
4.3.22
to be true, for all
T\in\mathbf{dg-cat}
we have
\operatorname{Re}(\Sing)(T)\simeq T
, and the functor
\Sing
is fully faithful.

Proof.

Let
T
be a dg-category. We construct, with the aforementioned methods, a
c\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}}
-hypercover of
T
,
T_{i}\to T
. By Lemma
4.5.5
, we know that the image by
\Sing
of this morphism is a DK-equivalence. We then have the following diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Re}(\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Re}(\Sing(T))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hocolim(\operatorname{Re}(\Sing(T_{i})))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hocolim(T_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

Assuming Hypothesis
4.3.22
to be true, we know that the morphism
\hocolim(\Sing(T_{i}))\to\Sing(T)
is a weak equivalence for the complete dg-Segal model structure. So
f
is a weak equivalence on the homotopy categories. On the other hand, because
\operatorname{Re}
is a left hand adjoint, we know we can commute with the homotopy colimit, so
g
is also a weak equivalence. Lastly, we have proven in Proposition
4.5.1
that
\Sing
is fully faithful over free dg-categories, and as all
T_{i}
are free dg-categories by construction, we have that
\operatorname{Re}(\Sing(T_{i}))\simeq T_{i}
for all
T_{i}
and the morphism
\phi
is also a weak equivalence. By the two out of three condition on model categories, that means that the morphism
\zeta
is also a weak equivalence.

That gives us the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hocolim(T_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Re}(\Sing(T_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

The vertical morphism is a weak equivalence by Proposition
4.4.14
, and
\zeta
is a weak equivalence too. By the two out of three condition, we have that
\operatorname{Re}(\Sing(T))\to T
is a weak equivalence. The functor
\Sing
is fully faithful and we have finished out proof.

∎

We finally know the functor is fully faithful! We can now go back to proving it is essentially surjective.

4.6
The functor
Sing
is essentially surjective

All that is left for us to do is to prove that every functor that satisfies the dg-Segal conditions is isomorphic to an object of the form
\Sing(T)
. In order to do that, we are going to use hypercovers again: for all functor
F
that satisfies the dg-Segal conditions, we will construct a hypercover of a dg-category
T
whose image by the
Sing
functor is a hypercover of
F
. So first we need to define what a hypercover of
F
is.

Definition 4.6.1
.

Let
F,G\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
. We say that a morphism
f:F\to G
is a
dg-Segal epimorphism
if we have the following two conditions:

1.

The morphism
f
is an isomorphism on the objects,
\pi_{0}(F(k))\to\pi_{0}(G(k))
.

2.

For all
x,y,\in\pi_{0}(F(k))
, the induced morphism
F_{(x,y)}\to G_{(x,y)}
is a split epimorphism.

Now that we have our definition of what epimorphism we want, we can define our hypercover. It isn’t complicated: we will essentially use the same definition we used last time, utilizing dg-Segal epimorphisms instead of
M_{0}
-epimorphisms.

Definition 4.6.2
.

Let
F\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
a functor that satisfies the dg-Segal conditions, and
F_{*}\to F
an augmented simplicial object in
\mathbf{dg-Segal}
. We say that
F_{*}
is
a dg-Segal-hypercover of
F
if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
the functor

F_{n}\to F_{*}^{\partial\Delta^{n}}

is a dg-Segal epimorphism. In other words, using Proposition
3.3.16
,
F_{*}\to F
is a dg-Segal-hypercover if

F_{0}\to F

is a dg-Segal epimorphism and for all
n\geq 1

F_{n}\to(\mathbb{R}\cosk_{n-1}\sk_{n-1}F)_{n}

is a dg-Segal epimorphism.

Now that we have our definition of hypercover, our objective will be to construct for all
F
a hypercover made of functors of the form
\Sing(T_{i})
with
T_{i}
a free dg-category. Following exactly the proof of Theorem
4.4.6
, this would be the point where we would prove that for every
F\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
, there exists a free dg-category
T
such that
\Sing(T)\to F
is a dg-Segal epimorphism. Unluckily for us, while that result is undoubtedly true, utilizing it would later make the objects in our hypercover explode, and we do not want that. So we are going to fix the objects first.

Notation 4.6.3
.

We denote the functor
\Sing(k)\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
by
\underline{k}
.

Definition 4.6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a set. We define
the category of dg-Segal spaces with fixed objects over
\mathcal{O}
to be the full subcategory of
\coprod_{\mathcal{O}}\underline{k}/\mathbf{dg-Segal}
of
F
dg-Segal spaces such that the morphism
\mathcal{O}\to\pi_{0}(F(k))
is an isomorphism, and we denote it by
\mathbf{dg-Segal}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Definition 4.6.5
.

Let
\Phi:\mathbf{dg-Segal}_{\mathcal{O}}\to\mathbf{dg-Segal}
be the forgetful functor. We define a dg-Segal epimorphism on
\mathbf{dg-Segal}_{\mathcal{O}}
to be a morphism
f
in
\mathbf{dg-Segal}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\Phi(f)
is a dg-Segal epimorphism.

Remark 4.6.6
.

It is easy to see that the first condition of the dg-Segal epimorphism is always true in
\mathbf{dg-Segal}_{\mathcal{O}}
. Consequently, we won’t have to check that condition as long as we are working on fixed objects.

Lemma 4.6.7
.

Let
F\in\mathbf{dg-Segal}
be a functor that satisfies the Segal conditions. We fix a set
\mathcal{O}=\pi_{0}(F(k))
. There exists a dg-category with fixed objects
T\in\mathbf{dg-cat}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\Sing(T)\to F
is a dg-Segal epimorphism in
\mathbf{dg-Segal}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Proof.

Most of the work for the construction of this dg-category has already been done, and we only have to put it together. We define a graph
G
as follows:

—

\Ob(G)=\mathcal{O}
.

—

For all
x,y\in\mathcal{O}
, we have that
G(x,y)=F(x,y)
, the representing object of
F_{(x,y)}
.

We define then
T=L(G)
as being the free category constructed from
G
.

Let us now prove that the morphism
\Sing(T)\to F
